If the distance from the vertex to the focus is 4 units upwards along the y-axis, then the directrix should be an equal distance on the opposite side of the vertex. That means the directrix will be a horizontal line below the vertex at y = -4.

Now, I need to recall the standard form of a parabola's equation when it opens upward or downward. The general form is:

(y - k) = (1/(4p)) * (x - h)^2

Where (h, k) is the vertex, and p is the distance from the vertex to the focus (or from the vertex to the directrix). In this case, the vertex is (0, 0), so h=0 and k=0. The value of p should be the distance between the vertex and the focus, which is 4.

Plugging these into the equation:

y = (1/(4*4)) * x^2

Simplifying that:

y = (1/16)x^2

Another version of the standard form is:

In this case, since we have a vertical axis of symmetry (because focus and vertex are on the y-axis), the parabola opens upward. So plugging in the values: x^2 = 4*4*y => x^2 = 16y.

To find the **vertex** of the parabola represented by the equation:

\[ y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \]

we can use the method of completing the square. Here's a step-by-step solution:

---

### Step 1: Start with the given equation
\[ y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \]

---

### Step 2: Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms
\[ y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5 \]

---

### Step 3: Complete the square inside the parentheses

To complete the square for \( x^2 + 2x \):
1. Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \)
2. Square it: \( (1)^2 = 1 \)

Add and subtract this square inside the equation:
\[ y = 3\left[(x^2 + 2x + 1) - 1\right] + 5 \]

---

### Step 4: Simplify the equation
\[ y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5 \]
\[ y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \]

---

### Step 5: Identify the vertex form of a parabola

The vertex form of a parabola is:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]
where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.

Comparing this with our equation:
- \( h = -1 \)
- \( k = 2 \)

---

### Final Answer
The **vertex** of the parabola is:

\[
\boxed{-1}
\]

Step-by-Step Explanation:
The original equation is \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \). To convert it into vertex form:

1. Rearrange terms to isolate the quadratic and linear terms of x: 
   \( x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12 \).

2. Complete the square for x by adding (4/2)^2 = 4 to both sides:
   \( x^2 - 4x + 4 = -8y - 8 \).

3. Factor the left side and simplify the right side:
   \( (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \).

The equation is now in vertex form, with the vertex at (2, -1).

Given the problem where we need to find the equation of a "parabola" with vertex at \( (0, 3) \) and directrix at \( y = 3 \), we start by understanding that a parabola is defined as the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point (focus) and a fixed line (directrix).

1. **Vertex and Directrix**: The vertex is given as \( (0, 3) \). The directrix is the horizontal line \( y = 3 \).
2. **Focus Consideration**: For a standard parabola opening upwards or downwards, the focus would be at some point along the vertical line through the vertex. However, since the directrix is given as \( y = 3 \) and the vertex is also on this line, we consider if the parabola can open normally.
3. **Degenerate Case**: If the directrix coincides with the level of the vertex, it implies that the distance from the focus to the directrix (which is zero) would make the parameter \( p \) (the distance from the vertex to the focus) zero. This results in a degenerate parabola.
4. **Equation Derivation**: For any point \( (x, y) \) on this "parabola", the distance to the focus must equal the distance to the directrix. However, since both distances simplify such that only points along the vertical line through the vertex satisfy the condition:
   - The equation simplifies to \( x = 0 \).

Thus, despite being called a parabola, this specific case reduces to the degenerate case where the "parabola" is simply the vertical line passing through the vertex.

\boxed{x = 0}

The given equation of the parabola is in standard form y = (x - 1)^2 + 5. 

- **Vertex (V):** From the equation, h = 1 and k = 5, so V = (1, 5).
  
- **Focus (F):** The distance from the vertex to the focus is 1/(4a). Here, a = 1, so this distance is 1/4. Since the parabola opens upwards, F is above the vertex: (1, 5 + 1/4) = (1, 21/4).
  
- **Directrix (D):** This line is below the vertex and at a distance of 1/(4a). So D is y = 5 - 1/4 = 19/4.

First, I need to find a quadratic equation that passes through three given points: (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4). A quadratic equation has the general form y = ax² + bx + c.

Since one of the points is (0, 4), substituting x = 0 into the equation gives y = c. Therefore, c = 4.

Next, I'll substitute the remaining two points into the equation to create a system of equations:

For (2, 8):
8 = a(2)² + b(2) + 4
Simplifying: 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → 4a + 2b = 4

For (-1, 3):
3 = a(-1)² + b(-1) + 4
Simplifying: 3 = a - b + 4 → a - b = -1

Now I have the system:
4a + 2b = 4  
a - b = -1

I'll solve this system to find the values of a and b.

First, from the second equation, express a in terms of b: a = b - 1

Substitute a into the first equation:
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4  
Expanding: 4b - 4 + 2b = 4 → 6b - 4 = 4 → 6b = 8 → b = 8/6 = 4/3

Now, substitute b back into a = b -1:
a = (4/3) - 1 = 1/3

With a and c known, the quadratic equation is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

To express this in standard form without fractions, multiply all terms by 3:
3y = x² + 4x + 12  
Which simplifies to: x² + 4x - 3y + 12 = 0

Let's determine the quadratic equation that passes through the points \((2, 8)\), \((-1, 3)\), and \((0, 4)\).

A general quadratic equation is given by:
\[
y = ax^2 + bx + c
\]

**Step 1: Substitute the point (0, 4)**
\[
4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \quad \Rightarrow \quad c = 4
\]

**Step 2: Substitute the remaining points into the equation**

For \((2, 8)\):
\[
8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 4a + 2b = 4 \quad (1)
\]

For \((-1, 3)\):
\[
3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \quad \Rightarrow \quad a - b = -1 \quad (2)
\]

**Step 3: Solve the system of equations**

From equation (2):
\[
a = b - 1
\]

Substitute \(a\) into equation (1):
\[
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \\
4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \\
6b = 8 \quad \Rightarrow \quad b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Now, find \(a\):
\[
a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Final Equation:**
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4
\]
To express it without fractions:
\[
3y = x^2 + 4x + 12 \\
x^2 + 4x - 3y + 12 = 0
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{x^2 + 4x - 3y + 12 = 0}
\]

Okay, I need to find the equation of a parabola given its vertex and directrix. The vertex is (2, -1) and the directrix is y = -4. 

First, remember that a parabola is the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point called the focus and a fixed line called the directrix. Since I know the vertex and the directrix, I can find the focus.

The vertex form of a parabola depends on whether it opens up, down, left, or right. The directrix is a horizontal line (y = -4), which means the parabola must open either upward or downward. 

In the case where the directrix is below the vertex and the parabola opens upward, the distance between the vertex and the directrix is equal to 2p, where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also from the vertex to the directrix). Wait, actually, if the parabola opens upward, then p would be positive. 

Wait, let me clarify: The formula for a parabola opening upwards is (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k), where (h,k) is the vertex and p is the distance from the vertex to the focus. Since the directrix is below the vertex, it's y = k - p.

The given equation is a circle in general form. To find its center and radius, we complete the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange the terms to group like variables:**
   \[
   (x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \( x \)-terms:**
   - Take half of 6, which is 3.
   - Square it to get 9.
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
     \[
     (x^2 + 6x + 9) = (x + 3)^2
     \]

4. **Complete the square for the \( y \)-terms:**
   - Take half of -4, which is -2.
   - Square it to get 4.
   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
     \[
     (y^2 - 4y + 4) = (y - 2)^2
     \]

5. **Adjust the equation by adding these squares:**
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 12 + 9 + 4
   \]
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

6. **Identify the center and radius:**
   - The standard form of a circle is:
     \[
     (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
     \]
   - Comparing, \( h = -3 \) and \( k = 2 \), so the center is at (-3, 2).
   - The radius \( r \) is the square root of 25, which is 5.

**Final Answer:**
- Center: (-3, 2)
- Radius: 5

   \[
   (x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
   \]

2. Complete the square for \(x\):
   - Coefficient of \(x\) is -4. Half of that is -2, squaring gives 4.
   - Add and subtract 4:
     \[

     \]

   - Coefficient of \(y\) is 2. Half of that is 1, squaring gives 1.

     \[

   \[

   \]
   
5. Simplify:
   \[

   \]

$$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2$$

Okay, I need to find the intersection points between the line y = x + 3 and the circle x² + y² = 17. 

First, since both equations involve x and y, I can substitute y from the line equation into the circle's equation.

So substituting y = x + 3 into x² + y² = 17 gives me:
x² + (x + 3)² = 17

Expanding the squared term: 
(x + 3)² = x² + 6x + 9

Now, plug that back into the equation:
x² + x² + 6x + 9 = 17
Combine like terms:
2x² + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0
Simplify:
2x² + 6x - 8 = 0

Divide both sides by 2 to make it simpler:
x² + 3x - 4 = 0

Now, solve the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula. The discriminant D is b² - 4ac.

Here a=1, b=3, c=-4.
D = 9 + 16 = 25

Since the discriminant is positive, there are two real solutions:

The given equation can be rewritten in the standard form of a circle's equation by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange terms to group \(x\) and \(y\) terms together:**
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \(x\)-terms:**
   - Take half of \(-8\), which is \(-4\).
   - Square it to get \(16\).
   \[
   x^2 - 8x + 16 = (x - 4)^2
   \]

4. **Complete the square for the \(y\)-terms:**
   - Take half of \(-6\), which is \(-3\).
   - Square it to get \(9\).
   \[
   y^2 - 6y + 9 = (y - 3)^2
   \]

5. **Add the completed squares to both sides:**
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = -20 + 16 + 9
   \]
   
6. **Simplify the right side:**
   \[
   -20 + 16 + 9 = 5
   \]

7. **Final equation in standard form:**
   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (\sqrt{5})^2
   \]

**Conclusion:**

- **Center of the circle:** \((4, 3)\)
- **Radius of the circle:** \(\sqrt{5}\)

It's impossible to determine the unique equation of the ellipse with only the information provided. Additional conditions or points are needed to solve for all parameters (h, k, a, and b).

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   - Given maximum x-coordinate = 6.
   - Given minimum y-coordinate = 4.

2. **Ellipse Basics:**
   - An ellipse equation in standard form is ((X-h)^2)/a² + ((Y-k)^2)/b² = 1, where (h,k) is the center, a is the semi-major axis, and b is the semi-minor axis.
   
3. **Identify Key Points:**
   - The point with maximum x-coordinate lies on the ellipse at (6, k).
   - The point with minimum y-coordinate lies on the ellipse at (h, 4).

4. **Formulate Equations:**
   - From max x: h + a = 6.
   - From min y: k - b = 4.

5. **Express Parameters in Terms of Center Coordinates:**
   - a = 6 - h
   - b = k - 4

6. **Substitute Back into the Ellipse Equation:**
   - ((X-h)^2)/((6 - h)²) + ((Y -k)^2)/( (k - 4)² ) = 1.

7. **Conclusion:**
   - Without knowing either h or k, there are infinitely many ellipses that satisfy these conditions.
   - Therefore, the ellipse's equation cannot be uniquely determined with only the given information.

Final Answer:
It is impossible to determine a unique equation for the ellipse without additional information such as another point on the ellipse or specific relationships between its axes.

***********************<think>
First, I identify the equation as that of an ellipse given by \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \).

Comparing it with the standard form, I determine that:
- \( a^2 = 22 \) so \( a = \sqrt{22} \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \) so \( b = \sqrt{16} = 4 \)

Next, I calculate the eccentricity using the formula \( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \):
\( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{16}{22}} = \sqrt{\frac{6}{22}} = \frac{\sqrt{33}}{11} \)

Finally, the foci are located at \( (\pm c, 0) \), where \( c = a \times e \):
\( c = \sqrt{22} \times \frac{\sqrt{33}}{11} = \sqrt{6} \)
Thus, the coordinates of the foci are \( (-\sqrt{6}, 0) \) and \( (\sqrt{6}, 0) \).
</think>

To determine the **foci** of the ellipse given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{22} + \frac{y^2}{16} = 1
\]

We follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Standard Form of the Ellipse Equation

The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where:
- \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

### Step 2: Determine \( a \) and \( b \)

Comparing with the given equation:

\[
a^2 = 22 \quad \Rightarrow \quad a = \sqrt{22}
\]
\[
b^2 = 16 \quad \Rightarrow \quad b = \sqrt{16} = 4
\]

### Step 3: Calculate the Distance from the Center to Each Focus (\( c \))

The distance \( c \) is given by:

\[
c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2}
\]
\[
c = \sqrt{22 - 16} = \sqrt{6}
\]

### Step 4: Determine the Coordinates of the Foci

Since the ellipse is oriented along the x-axis (as \( a > b \)), the foci are located at:

\[
(\pm c, 0) = (\pm \sqrt{6}, 0)
\]

**Final Answer:**

The foci of the ellipse are at **(-√6, 0)** and **(√6, 0)**.

The given equation represents an ellipse with semi-major axis \( a = 6 \) and semi-minor axis \( b = 3 \). The eccentricity \( e \) is calculated using \( e = \sqrt{1 - (b/a)^2} \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the standard form of the ellipse equation**:  
   The given equation is \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), which fits the standard form \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \) where \( a > b \).

2. **Determine semi-major axis (a) and semi-minor axis (b)**:  
   - \( a^2 = 36 \Rightarrow a = 6 \)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \Rightarrow b = 3 \)

3. **Calculate the eccentricity**:  
   The formula for eccentricity (\( e \)) is:
   \[
   e = \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^2}
   \]
   Plugging in the values:
   \[
   e = \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{3}{6}\right)^2} = \sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2} = \sqrt{1 - \frac{1}{4}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The eccentricity of the ellipse is \( e = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

\]

\[

\]

\]

\]
\[
1600 = 2500 - b^2
\]
\[
b^2 = 900 \Rightarrow b = 30
\]

Given the parametric equations \( x = \sqrt{\sin \theta} \) and \( y = \sqrt{\cos \theta} \), we need to find the relationship between \( x \) and \( y \) that eliminates \( \theta \).

First, we express \( \sin \theta \) and \( \cos \theta \) in terms of \( x \) and \( y \):
\[ \sin \theta = x^2 \]
\[ \cos \theta = y^2 \]

Using the Pythagorean identity:
\[ \sin^2 \theta + \cos^2 \theta = 1 \]

Substituting the expressions for \( \sin \theta \) and \( \cos \theta \):
\[ (x^2)^2 + (y^2)^2 = 1 \]
\[ x^4 + y^4 = 1 \]

Thus, the curve on which all such points lie is given by the equation:
\[
\boxed{x^4 + y^4 = 1}
\]

To determine the area of an ellipse defined by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), I start by identifying the standard form of an ellipse, which is \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \). Here, (h,k) represents the center of the ellipse, and a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

In this case, the given equation is already in standard form with \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \), indicating that the center of the ellipse is at the origin (0,0). Comparing the denominators under x² and y² to a² and b² respectively, I find that \( a^2 = 4 \) which means \( a = 2 \), and \( b^2 = 9 \) leading to \( b = 3 \).

Next, I apply the formula for the area of an ellipse, which is \( \pi \times a \times b \). Substituting the values, the area becomes \( \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \).
</think>

To determine the area of the ellipse defined by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Standard Form of an Ellipse
The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

### Step 2: Compare with the Given Equation
Comparing the given equation:
\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]
with the standard form, we identify:
- \( a^2 = 4 \) ⇒ \( a = 2 \)
- \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)

### Step 3: Calculate the Area of the Ellipse
The area (\( A \)) of an ellipse is given by:
\[
A = \pi \times a \times b
\]
Substituting the values:
\[
A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi
\]

### Final Answer: {Final Answer: "6π"}

To determine the asymptotes and foci of the hyperbola \( \frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \):

1. **Identify Hyperbola Parameters**:
   - The equation is in standard form for a horizontally oriented hyperbola centered at (0,0).
   - Here, \( a^2 = 9 \) so \( a = 3 \), and \( b^2 = 16 \) so \( b = 4 \).

2. **Asymptotes**:
   - The equations for the asymptotes of such hyperbolas are \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x \).
   - Plugging in, we get \( y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \).

3. **Foci**:
   - The distance from the center to each focus is given by \( c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = 5 \).
   - Since it's a horizontal hyperbola, foci are at (±c,0), so they're located at (5,0) and (-5,0).

**Final Answer:**
- **Asymptotes**: \( y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \)
- **Foci**: At points (5,0) and (-5,0)

Given the foci are at (0, ±√10), we know \( c = \sqrt{10} \) and thus \( c^2 = 10 \). For hyperbolas, \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \).

The point (2,3) lies on the hyperbola, so substituting into the equation gives:
\[ \frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \]

We now have two equations:
1. \( a^2 + b^2 = 10 \)
2. \( \frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1 \)

Solving these simultaneously by substituting \( b^2 = 10 - a^2 \) into the second equation leads to:
\[ 9/a^2 - 4/(10 - a^2) = 1 \]

This simplifies to solving for \( A = a^2 \):
\[ (90 - 13A)/(A(10 - A)) = 1 \]
Which results in the quadratic equation:
\[ A^2 - 23A + 90 = 0 \]

Factoring gives \( A = 5 \) or \( A = 18 \). Since \( B = b^2 = 10 - A \), only \( A = 5 \) yields a positive value for \( B \), giving both \( a^2 = 5 \) and \( b^2 = 5 \).

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:
\[ \frac{y^2}{5} - \frac{x^2}{5} = 1 \]

Alright, so I need to find the equation of a hyperbola given certain conditions. The user mentioned an axis-aligned hyperbola with asymptotes having slopes ±2 and transverse axis length of 4 units. Let me break this down step by step.

Since the user mentioned "افقی" which translates to horizontal axis-aligned, I'm assuming it's a horizontal hyperbola. So, the standard form would be:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, 'a' is the distance from the center to each vertex along the transverse axis, and 'b' relates to the conjugate axis. The asymptotes of a horizontal hyperbola are given by:

\[
y = \pm \frac{b}{a} x
\]

The user provided the slopes of the asymptotes as ±2. So, setting up the equation from this information:

\[
\frac{b}{a} = 2 \implies b = 2a
\]

Next, the transverse axis length is given as 4 units. The transverse axis in a horizontal hyperbola is along the x-axis and its length is 2a (from -a to +a). Therefore:

\[
2a = 4 \implies a = 2
\]

Now that I have 'a', I can find 'b' using the earlier relation:

\[
b = 2a = 2*2 = 4
\]

So, plugging these values back into the standard hyperbola equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{(2)^2} - \frac{y^2}{(4)^2} = 1 \implies \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1
\]

To present this neatly, I'll write it without denominators:

\[
x^2/4 - y^2/16 = 1
\]
</think>

The equation of the hyperbola is determined using the given slopes of asymptotes and the length of the transverse axis. For a horizontal hyperbola with asymptotes \(y = \pm 2x\) and a transverse axis length of 4, we find:

- The slope relationship gives \(b/a = 2\), so \(b = 2a\).
- The transverse axis length is \(2a = 4\), giving \(a = 2\).
- Substituting back, \(b = 4\).

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1

To find the **vertex** of the parabola defined by the equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Coefficients
The given quadratic equation is in the standard form:
\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]
where:
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 6 \)
- \( c = 5 \)

### Step 2: Use the Vertex Formula
The **vertex** of a parabola given by \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \) can be found using the formula for the vertex coordinates:
\[ (h, k) \]
where:
- \( h = -\frac{b}{2a} \)
- \( k \) is the value of \( y \) when \( x = h \)

### Step 3: Calculate the x-coordinate (\( h \))
Substitute the values of \( a \) and \( b \) into the formula for \( h \):
\[ h = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -\frac{6}{6} = -1 \]

### Step 4: Calculate the y-coordinate (\( k \))
Now, substitute \( x = -1 \) back into the original equation to find \( y \):
\[ y = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 \]
\[ y = 3(1) - 6 + 5 \]
\[ y = 3 - 6 + 5 \]
\[ y = 2 \]

### Step 5: Write the Vertex Coordinates
The vertex of the parabola is at:
\[ (h, k) = (-1, 2) \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\{-1, 2\}}
\]

To find the center and radius, I need to rewrite this equation in the standard form by completing the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

For the \( x \)-terms: Take \( x^2 + 6x \), complete the square by adding and subtracting \( (6/2)^2 = 9 \).

For the \( y \)-terms: Take \( y^2 - 4y \), complete the square by adding and subtracting \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \).

After completing the squares, the equation becomes:

To determine the center and radius of the circle given by the equation:

\[ x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \]

we will rewrite the equation in the standard form of a circle's equation, which is:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Group like terms:**

   Start by grouping the \(x\)-terms and \(y\)-terms together:

   \[
   x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12
   \]

2. **Complete the square for \(x\):**

   Take the coefficient of \(x\), which is 6, divide by 2 to get 3, and then square it to get \(9\). Add and subtract this value inside the equation:

   \[
   (x^2 + 6x + 9) - 9
   \]

3. **Complete the square for \(y\):**

   Take the coefficient of \(y\), which is -4, divide by 2 to get -2, and then square it to get \(4\). Add and subtract this value inside the equation:

   \[
   (y^2 - 4y + 4) - 4
   \]

4. **Rewrite the equation with completed squares:**

   Substitute the completed squares back into the original equation:

   \[
   (x^2 + 6x + 9) + (y^2 - 4y + 4) = 12 + 9 + 4
   \]

   Simplify:

   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

5. **Identify the center and radius:**

   From the standard form, we can see that:
   
   - The center of the circle is at \((-3, 2)\)
   - The radius \(r\) is \(\sqrt{25} = 5\)

### Final Answer:

The center of the circle is \((-3, 2)\) and the radius is \(5\).

First, I need to find the equation of a circle that passes through three given points: (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1).

To determine the equation of the circle, I'll use the general form of a circle's equation:
\[ x^2 + y^2 + Dx + Ey + F = 0 \]
where \( D \), \( E \), and \( F \) are constants that I need to find.

Next, I'll substitute each of the given points into this general equation to create a system of linear equations. 

For the point (1, 1):
\[ 1^2 + 1^2 + D(1) + E(1) + F = 0 \]
which simplifies to:
\[ 1 + 1 + D + E + F = 0 \]
or
\[ D + E + F = -2 \]

For the point (2, -3):
\[ 2^2 + (-3)^2 + D(2) + E(-3) + F = 0 \]
which simplifies to:
\[ 4 + 9 + 2D - 3E + F = 0 \]
or
\[ 2D - 3E + F = -13 \]

For the point (5, -1):
\[ 5^2 + (-1)^2 + D(5) + E(-1) + F = 0 \]
which simplifies to:
\[ 25 + 1 + 5D - E + F = 0 \]
or
\[ 5D - E + F = -26 \]

Now, I have a system of three linear equations:
1. \( D + E + F = -2 \)
2. \( 2D - 3E + F = -13 \)
3. \( 5D - E + F = -26 \)

To solve this system, I'll use the method of elimination. 

First, I'll subtract equation 1 from equation 2 to eliminate \( F \):
\[ (2D - 3E + F) - (D + E + F) = -13 - (-2) \]
Simplifying:
\[ D - 4E = -11 \]

Next, I'll subtract equation 1 from equation 3:
\[ (5D - E + F) - (D + E + F) = -26 - (-2) \]
Simplifying:
\[ 4D - 2E = -24 \]

Now, I have two new equations:
A. \( D - 4E = -11 \)
B. \( 4D - 2E = -24 \)

I'll solve equation A for \( D \):
\[ D = 4E - 11 \]

Substituting this expression into equation B:
\[ 4(4E - 11) - 2E = -24 \]
Simplifying:
\[ 16E - 44 - 2E = -24 \]
\[ 14E - 44 = -24 \]
Adding 44 to both sides:
\[ 14E = 20 \]
Dividing by 14:
\[ E = \frac{20}{14} = \frac{10}{7} \]

Now, substituting \( E = \frac{10}{7} \) back into equation A to find \( D \):
\[ D - 4\left(\frac{10}{7}\right) = -11 \]
Simplifying:
\[ D - \frac{40}{7} = -11 \]
\[ D = -11 + \frac{40}{7} \]
Converting -11 to sevenths:
\[ D = -\frac{77}{7} + \frac{40}{7} \]
\[ D = -\frac{37}{7} \]

Finally, I'll substitute \( D = -\frac{37}{7} \) and \( E = \frac{10}{7} \) into equation 1 to find \( F \):
\[ -\frac{37}{7} + \frac{10}{7} + F = -2 \]
Simplifying:
\[ -\frac{27}{7} + F = -2 \]
Adding \(\frac{27}{7}\) to both sides:
\[ F = -2 + \frac{27}{7} \]
Converting -2 to sevenths:
\[ F = -\frac{14}{7} + \frac{27}{7} \]
\[ F = \frac{13}{7} \]

Now that I have \( D = -\frac{37}{7} \), \( E = \frac{10}{7} \), and \( F = \frac{13}{7} \), the equation of the circle in general form is:
\[ x^2 + y^2 - \frac{37}{7}x + \frac{10}{7}y + \frac{13}{7} = 0 \]

To present this more neatly, I'll multiply every term by 7 to eliminate the denominators:
\[ 7x^2 + 7y^2 - 37x + 10y + 13 = 0 \]

To find the center and radius of the circle given by the equation:

\[ x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Rewrite the Equation:**
   Start with the given equation:
   \[ x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \]

2. **Group Like Terms:**
   Group the \(x\) terms and \(y\) terms together:
   \[ (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20 \]

3. **Complete the Square for \(x\):**
   To complete the square for the \(x\) terms, take half of the coefficient of \(x\) which is \(-4\), square it to get \(16\), and add it inside the equation:
   \[ x^2 - 8x + 16 = (x - 4)^2 \]

4. **Complete the Square for \(y\):**
   Similarly, take half of the coefficient of \(y\) which is \(-3\), square it to get \(9\), and add it inside the equation:
   \[ y^2 - 6y + 9 = (y - 3)^2 \]

5. **Add the Squares to Both Sides:**
   Add the squares to both sides of the equation to maintain equality:
   \[
   (x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9
   \]
   Simplifying the right side:
   \[ (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5 \]

6. **Identify the Center and Radius:**
   The equation is now in standard form for a circle:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Where:
   - \(h = 4\)
   - \(k = 3\)
   - \(r^2 = 5\), so the radius \(r = \sqrt{5}\)

**Final Answer:**

The circle has a center at (4, 3) and a radius of √5.

The general form of a horizontally opening hyperbola is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, \(a\) represents the distance from the center to each vertex. Given that the vertices are at (±4, 0), I can see that \(a = 4\). So, plugging this into the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Simplifying, it becomes:

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Now, I need to find the value of \(b^2\) using the information about the asymptotes. The equations of the asymptotes for a hyperbola in this form are:

\[
y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x
\]

We're told that the asymptotes are y = ±(1/3)x, which means that \( \frac{b}{a} = \frac{1}{3} \). Since we already know \( a = 4 \), I can set up the equation:

\[
\frac{b}{4} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

To solve for \(b\), multiply both sides by 4:

\[
b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

But since we need \(b^2\) in our hyperbola equation, let's square this value:

\[
b^2 = \left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^2 = \frac{16}{9}
\]

Now that I have both \(a^2 = 16\) and \(b^2 = 16/9\), I can substitute these back into the standard hyperbola equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{\frac{16}{9}} = 1
\]

The hyperbola's equation is determined by its vertices and asymptotes. With vertices at (±4, 0), we know \(a = 4\). The asymptotes \(y = \pm \frac{1}{3}x\) give us \(\frac{b}{a} = \frac{1}{3}\), leading to \(b = \frac{4}{3}\) and thus \(b^2 = \frac{16}{9}\). Substituting these into the standard form of a horizontally opening hyperbola, we get:

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{y^2}{\frac{16}{9}} = 1
\]

Which simplifies to:

\[
\frac{x^2}{16} - \frac{9y^2}{16} = 1
\]

\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{16} - \dfrac{9 y^2}{16} = 1}

Given the foci at \( (\pm \sqrt{10}, 0) \), we know the hyperbola opens along the x-axis. The distance from the center to each focus is \( c = \sqrt{10} \).

The standard form of a hyperbola opening horizontally is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \).

From the given foci, we have \( c^2 = 10 \). We also know that for any point on the hyperbola, the absolute difference of distances to the two foci is constant and equal to \( 2a \).

We found that both \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \) are equal to 5 because:
\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \implies 10 = 5 + 5
\]

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{5} - \frac{y^2}{5} = 1
\]

The final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{5} - \dfrac{y^2}{5} = 1}
\]

First, since it's aligned with the axes, the standard form will be either (x²/a²) - (y²/b²) = 1 or (y²/b²) - (x²/a²) = 1. The asymptotes for a hyperbola centered at the origin are given by y = ±(b/a)x if it's opening left and right, or y = ±(a/b)x if it's opening up and down.

The given problem involves finding the equation of a parabola with its vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4). Since the focus is above the vertex, the parabola opens upwards. The standard form for such a parabola is \(x^2 = 4py\), where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

Given that the focus is at (0, 4), we have:
- Vertex: (0, 0)
- Focus: (0, 4)
- Therefore, \(p = 4\)

Substituting into the standard form:
\[x^2 = 4 \times 4y\]
Simplifying this gives:
\[x^2 = 16y\]

First, I recognize that the equation \( y^2 = 32x \) represents a parabola. To identify its key features, such as the focus and directrix, I'll compare it to the standard form of a horizontally opening parabola.

The general form is \( (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \), where the vertex is at point \( (h, k) \). In this case, the equation simplifies to \( y^2 = 32x \), so the vertex is at the origin, (0, 0).

Next, I'll determine the value of \( p \) by equating coefficients. Comparing \( 4p = 32 \), solving for \( p \) gives \( p = 8 \). This tells me that the focus is located 8 units to the right of the vertex along the axis of symmetry.

Therefore, the coordinates of the focus are (8, 0).

For the directrix, since it's a horizontal parabola opening to the right, the directrix will be a vertical line located at \( x = -p \). Substituting \( p = 8 \), the equation of the directrix is \( x = -8 \).
</think>

**Solution:**

The given equation is:
\[ y^2 = 32x \]

This represents a parabola that opens to the right. 

1. **Identify the Standard Form:**
   
   The standard form of a horizontally opening parabola is:
   \[ (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \]
   
   Comparing with the given equation, we have:
   \[
   y^2 = 32x \\
   (y - 0)^2 = 4 \times 8 \times (x - 0)
   \]
   
   So, \( h = 0 \), \( k = 0 \), and \( 4p = 32 \) which gives \( p = 8 \).

2. **Determine the Focus:**
   
   For a parabola that opens to the right:
   - The vertex is at \( (h, k) = (0, 0) \).
   - The focus is located at \( (h + p, k) \).
   
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   \text{Focus} = (0 + 8, 0) = (8, 0)
   \]

3. **Determine the Directrix:**
   
   For a parabola that opens to the right:
   - The directrix is given by \( x = h - p \).
   
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   \text{Directrix} = x = 0 - (-8) \\
                   = x = -8
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[ \boxed{\text{Focus}: (8, 0),\ \text{Directrix}: x = -8} \]

To find the **vertex form** of the given quadratic equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify Coefficients
Compare the equation with the standard form \( y = ax^2 + bx + c \):
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 6 \)
- \( c = 5 \)

### Step 2: Calculate the x-coordinate of the Vertex
The formula to find the vertex's x-coordinate is:
\[
x = -\frac{b}{2a}
\]
Plugging in the values:
\[
x = -\frac{6}{2 \times 3} = -1
\]

### Step 3: Calculate the y-coordinate of the Vertex
Substitute \( x = -1 \) back into the original equation to find the corresponding y-coordinate:
\[
y = 3(-1)^2 + 6(-1) + 5 = 3(1) - 6 + 5 = 3 - 6 + 5 = 2
\]

### Step 4: Write the Vertex Form
The vertex form of a quadratic equation is:
\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]
Where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex. Substituting the values we found:
\[
y = 3(x - (-1))^2 + 2 \\
y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]

### Final Answer
The vertex form of the equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \) is:

\[
\boxed{y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2}
\]

8 = 4a + 2b + 4

4 = 4a + 2b

2 = 2a + b

= 12/3 + 4  
= 4 + 4

- For (0,4): 4 = c
- Using (2,8): 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → 2a + b = 2
- Using (-1,3): 3 = a - b + 4 → a - b = -1

Solving these equations gives a = 1/3 and b = 4/3. Thus, the equation is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

Recognizing that both points share the same y-coordinate suggests a horizontal axis of symmetry. The standard form for such a parabola is \((y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)\), where \( (h, k) \) is the vertex and \( p \) determines the direction and width.

2. **Determine the value of p**: The focus is at (-2, 1). Since moving from x=-4 to x=-2 is a distance of +2 units along the x-axis, \( p = 2 \).
3. **Formulate the equation**: Plugging into the standard form:
   - \( (y - 1)^2 = 4*2*(x - (-4)) \)
   - Simplify to: \( (y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4) \)

Thus, the equation of the parabola is **\( (y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4) \)**.

Starting with the original equation:

I'll group the x terms and y terms together:
(x² + 6x) + (y² - 4y) = 12

Next, I complete the square for both groups. For the x terms, I take half of 6, which is 3, square it to get 9, and add it inside the equation. Similarly, for the y terms, I take half of -4, which is -2, square it to get 4, and add it as well.

**Solution:**

We are given the equation of a circle:
\[ x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \]

To find the center \((h, k)\) and the radius \(r\) of the circle, we'll rewrite the equation in the standard form:
\[ (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \]

**Step 1: Group Like Terms**

Group the \(x\)-terms and \(y\)-terms together:
\[
x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12
\]

**Step 2: Complete the Square for Both Variables**

- **For \(x\):**
  \[
  x^2 + 6x \quad \text{becomes} \quad (x + 3)^2 - 9
  \]
  
- **For \(y\):**
  \[
  y^2 - 4y \quad \text{becomes} \quad (y - 2)^2 - 4
  \]

**Step 3: Substitute Back into the Equation**

Substitute the completed squares back:
\[
(x + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
\]

Simplify:
\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12 \\
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

**Step 4: Identify the Center and Radius**

From the standard form, we can see that:
- The center \((h, k)\) is at **(-3, 2)**.
- The radius \(r\) is \(\sqrt{25} = 5\).

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\text{Center: } (-3, 2), \text{ Radius: } 5}
\]

Starting with the original equation:
\[ x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \]

I'll rearrange and group the like terms:
\[
(x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
\]

Next, I complete the square for the \( x \)-terms. Taking half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \(-4/2 = -2\), and squaring it gives \( (-2)^2 = 4 \). Similarly, for the \( y \)-terms, half of \( 2 \) is \( 1 \), and squaring it yields \( 1^2 = 1 \).

I add these squares to both sides of the equation:
\[
(x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1
\]
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]

Now, the equation is in the standard form of a circle:

(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2

To convert the given equation of the circle into its standard form, follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \]

**Step 1: Rearrange and Group Like Terms**

Move all terms to one side:
\[
x^2 - 4x + y^2 + 2y = 11
\]

**Step 2: Complete the Square for \( x \) and \( y \)**

- For the \( x \)-terms (\( x^2 - 4x \)):
  
  Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \(-\frac{4}{2} = -2\)
  
  Square this value: \((-2)^2 = 4\)
  
  Add and subtract 4:
  \[
  (x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4
  \]

- For the \( y \)-terms (\( y^2 + 2y \)):
  
  Take half of the coefficient of \( y \): \(\frac{2}{2} = 1\)
  
  Square this value: \(1^2 = 1\)
  
  Add and subtract 1:
  \[
  (y^2 + 2y + 1) - 1
  \]

**Step 3: Rewrite the Equation with Completed Squares**

Substitute back into the equation:
\[
(x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4 + (y^2 + 2y + 1) - 1 = 11
\]
Simplify:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 - 5 = 11
\]

**Step 4: Isolate the Squared Terms**

Add 5 to both sides:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the circle in standard form is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16}
\]

Next, looking at the y-component: (y + 2)^2. Wait, hold on a second. In the standard form, it's (y - k)^2. But here we have (y + 2), which can be rewritten as (y - (-2)). So that means k is -2. Therefore, the y-coordinate of the center is -2.

The given equation \((x - 1)^2 + (y + 2)^2 = 25\) represents a circle in standard form, where the center coordinates are derived from the terms subtracted inside the squared terms and the radius is found by taking the square root of the constant term.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form:** The equation \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\) represents a circle with center at \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\).

2. **Compare to Given Equation:**
   - Comparing \(x - 1\) with \(x - h\), we find that \(h = 1\).
   - Comparing \(y + 2\) (which is equivalent to \(y - (-2)\)) with \(y - k\), we find that \(k = -2\).

3. **Determine the Radius:**
   - The constant term on the right side of the equation is 25, so \(r^2 = 25\).
   - Taking the square root gives \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\).

4. **Final Answer:**
   - Center: (1, -2)
   - Radius: 5

To find the radius and center of the circle defined by the equation:

\[ x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Rearrange the Equation:**
   Start by grouping like terms.

   \[ (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20 \]

2. **Complete the Square for \( x \):**
   
   Take the coefficient of \( x \), which is -8, divide it by 2 to get -4, and then square it to get 16.

   Add and subtract 16 within the equation:

   \[ (x^2 - 8x + 16) - 16 = \text{(No change on the right side)} \]

3. **Complete the Square for \( y \):**
   
   Take the coefficient of \( y \), which is -6, divide it by 2 to get -3, and then square it to get 9.

   Add and subtract 9 within the equation:

   \[ (y^2 - 6y + 9) - 9 = \text{(No change on the right side)} \]

4. **Rewrite the Equation:**
   
   Now, rewrite the perfect squares and combine constants:

   \[
   (x - 4)^2 - 16 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 = -20 \\
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 - 25 = -20
   \]

   Add 25 to both sides:

   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
   \]

5. **Identify the Center and Radius:**
   
   The standard form of a circle's equation is:

   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
   Comparing with our equation:
   
   \[
   h = 4, \quad k = 3, \quad r^2 = 5
   \]
   
   Therefore:

   - **Center:** \( (4, 3) \)
   - **Radius:** \( \sqrt{5} \)

**Final Answer:**

The circle has a center at (4, 3) and a radius of √5.

**Solution:**

To determine the equation of a circle with center \((1, 2)\) that passes through the point \((4, 6)\), we follow these steps:

1. **General Equation of a Circle:**
   
   The general formula for the equation of a circle is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle and \(r\) is its radius.

2. **Determine the Radius:**
   
   The radius can be found using the distance formula between the center \((1, 2)\) and the point \((4, 6)\):
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
   \]
   
   Substituting the given points:
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

3. **Substitute into the General Equation:**
   
   Now, plug the center coordinates and the radius into the general equation:
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 5^2
   \]
   Simplifying further:
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

- **Major Axis (Horizontal):** Length = 10
- **Minor Axis:** Length = 6
- **Center:** (0, 0)

### Step-by-step Explanation:

1. **Determine the semi-major axis (a):**
   - The length of the major axis is twice the semi-major axis.
   - \( a = \frac{10}{2} = 5 \)

2. **Determine the semi-minor axis (b):**
   - The length of the minor axis is twice the semi-minor axis.
   - \( b = \frac{6}{2} = 3 \)

3. **Use the standard equation for an ellipse centered at (h, k) with a horizontal major axis:**
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]

   \]

   \[
   \frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

- The semi-minor axis \(b\) is the square root of 16, which simplates to 4.

**Solution:**

The given equation represents an ellipse:
\[
\frac{x^2}{22} + \frac{y^2}{16} = 1
\]

For an ellipse of the form \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\) where \(a > b\), the following properties apply:

1. **Major Axis:** Along the x-axis.
2. **Semi-major axis (\(a\)):** \(a = \sqrt{22}\)
3. **Semi-minor axis (\(b\)):** \(b = 4\) (since \(16 = b^2\))
4. **Distance from center to each focus (\(c\)):**
   \[
   c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} = \sqrt{22 - 16} = \sqrt{6}
   \]

Since the major axis is along the x-axis, the foci are located at:
\[
(\pm \sqrt{6}, 0)
\]

\[
\boxed{\text{Foci at } (\pm \sqrt{6}, 0)}

Eccentricity e is calculated using the formula e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to each focus. But first, I need to find c. For an ellipse, c² = a² - b². Plugging in the values, c² = 36 - 9 = 27. So, c is sqrt(27), which simplifies to 3*sqrt(3).

Now, with c known, I can calculate e. Since e = c/a, substituting gives e = (3√3)/6. Simplifying that, the 3 in the numerator and denominator cancels out, leaving e = √3/2.

The given equation represents an ellipse with a semi-major axis \( a = 6 \) and a semi-minor axis \( b = 3 \). The eccentricity \( e \) is calculated as:

\[
e = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a} = \frac{\sqrt{36 - 9}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{27}}{6} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\]

The eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

The given equation is that of an ellipse in standard form \(\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\). Here, \(a = 2\) and \(b = 3\), where \(a\) is the semi-minor axis and \(b\) is the semi-major axis. The area of an ellipse is given by \(A = \pi a b\).

Substituting the values:
\[ A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \]

To determine whether the statement "For every real number \( x \), if \( x + 2 = 5 \) then \( x^3 - x > 0 \)" is true, we break it down as follows:

1. Solve the equation \( x + 2 = 5 \). This gives \( x = 3 \).
2. Evaluate the expression \( x^3 - x \) at \( x = 3 \):
   \[
   3^3 - 3 = 27 - 3 = 24
   \]
   Since 24 is greater than 0, the consequent holds true.
3. The implication "If \( P(x) \) then \( Q(x) \)" is vacuously true when there exists only one \( x \) making \( P(x) \) true and for which \( Q(x) \) also holds true.

Thus, the statement is true.

First, I recognize that the problem involves writing the equation of an ellipse based on its major and minor axes lengths and its center.

I know that the standard form of an ellipse's equation depends on whether the major axis is horizontal or vertical. In this case, since the major axis is vertical (y-axis), the standard form will be:

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
\]

Here, \( a \) is the semi-major axis length and \( b \) is the semi-minor axis length.

Given:
- The major axis has a length of 14, so \( a = 7 \).
- The minor axis has a length of 10, so \( b = 5 \).
- The center of the ellipse is at (4, -3).

Substituting these values into the standard form equation:

\[
\frac{(x - 4)^2}{5^2} + \frac{(y + 3)^2}{7^2} = 1
\]

Simplifying the denominators gives the final equation of the ellipse.
</think>

To write the equation of an ellipse with:
- **Major axis length**: 14 (vertical)
- **Minor axis length**: 10 (horizontal)
- **Center**: (4, -3)

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the standard form of an ellipse's equation:**
   
   The general equation for an ellipse centered at \((h, k)\) is:
   
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
   \]
   
   where:
   - \( a \): semi-major axis length (longer axis)
   - \( b \): semi-minor axis length (shorter axis)

2. **Determine the values of \( a \) and \( b \):**
   
   Given:
   - Major axis length = 14 ⇒ semi-major axis, \( a = \frac{14}{2} = 7 \)
   - Minor axis length = 10 ⇒ semi-minor axis, \( b = \frac{10}{2} = 5 \)

3. **Plug the values into the standard equation:**
   
   Substitute \( h = 4 \), \( k = -3 \), \( a = 7 \), and \( b = 5 \) into the ellipse equation:
   
   \[
   \frac{(x - 4)^2}{5^2} + \frac{(y + 3)^2}{7^2} = 1
   \]
   
   Simplify the denominators:
   
   \[
   \frac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \frac{(y + 3)^2}{49} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{(x-4)^2}{25}+\dfrac{(y+3)^2}{49}=1}

***********************<think>
First, I need to determine whether the given hyperbola equation is in its standard form. The equation provided is \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1\). Comparing this with the general form of a hyperbola centered at (h,k), which is \(\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1\) for a horizontally oriented hyperbola, I see that the given equation fits this standard form with \(h=0\), \(k=0\), \(a^2=9\), and \(b^2=16\).

Next, to find the asymptotes of the hyperbola, I use the formula for the asymptotes of a horizontally oriented hyperbola: \(y - k = \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h)\). Substituting the known values, where \(h=0\) and \(k=0\), the equations simplify to \(y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x\).

Finally, for the foci of the hyperbola, I use the formula \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2\). Calculating \(c^2\) gives \(9 + 16 = 25\), so \(c=5\). Since it's a horizontally oriented hyperbola centered at (0,0), the foci are located at \((\pm5, 0)\).

To determine the asymptotes and foci of the hyperbola given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Step 1: Identify the Standard Form**

The equation is in the standard form of a hyperbola centered at (0,0):

\[
\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, \(h = 0\) and \(k = 0\), so the center is at (0,0).

**Step 2: Determine Asymptotes**

For a horizontally oriented hyperbola, the equations of the asymptotes are:

\[
y - k = \pm \frac{b}{a}(x - h)
\]

Substituting \(h=0\), \(k=0\), \(a^2 = 9\) (so \(a = 3\)), and \(b^2 = 16\) (so \(b = 4\)):

\[
y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x
\]

**Step 3: Determine Foci**

The distance from the center to each focus is given by \(c\), where:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

Substituting \(a^2 = 9\) and \(b^2 = 16\):

\[
c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \\
c = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

Since the hyperbola is horizontal, the foci are located at:

\[
(0 + c, 0) \text{ and } (0 - c, 0) \\
(5, 0) \text{ and } (-5, 0)
\]

**Final Answer:**

The asymptotes of the hyperbola are \(y = \frac{4}{3}x\) and \(y = -\frac{4}{3}x\), and the foci are at (5,0) and (-5,0).

```json
  "Foci": [(5, 0), (-5, 0)]
}
```

1. **Identify Parameters**:

   - Here, \( h = 0 \) and \( k = 0 \), so the center is at (0, 0).

2. **Calculate a and b**:
   - \( a^2 = 4 \) → \( a = 2 \)
   - \( b^2 = 9 \) → \( b = 3 \)

3. **Find Foci**:
   - The distance to foci from the center is \( c \), where \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \).

   - Foci at (±√13, 0)

   - Equations are \( Y = \pm\frac{b}{a}X \).
   - Substituting values: \( Y = \pm\frac{3}{2}X \)
